Proof-Producing Translation of Functional Programs into a Time & Space Reasonable Model
Kevin Kappelmann, Fabian Huch, Lukas Stevens, Mohammad Abdulaziz

Abstract
We present a semi-automated framework to construct and reason about programs in a deeply-embedded while-language. The while-language we consider is a simple computation model that can simulate (and be simulated by) Turing Machines with a quadratic time and constant space blow-up. Our framework derives while-programs from functional programs written in a subset of Isabelle/HOL, namely tail-recursive functions with first-order arguments and algebraic datatypes. As far as we are aware, it is the first framework targeting a computation model that is reasonable in time and space from a complexity-theoretic perspective.



*label=(0)

1
Introduction

A large array of mathematical results has been formlised in interactive theorem provers.
One example is theoretical computer science, where extensive results on algorithm correctness
and computational objects, such as reductions, were proven.
Nonetheless, one type of results has proved to be particularly hard to formalise:
the construction and verification of algorithms modelled within a deeply embedded computation model, such as a Turing machine.
These constructions, however, are crucial to formalise the theory of efficient algorithms and computational complexity theory
as understood by practitioners of theoretical computing.
In these fields, most results show that an algorithm runs in polynomial time (worst-case, average case, etc.) when implemented as a program within a reasonable computation model, i.e. a model that can simulate Turing machines with a polynomial time and constant space overhead for
all
computations
[
36
]
.
Previous work on reasoning about concrete programs within a deeply embedded computation model did so in one of two ways:

1.

Automated synthesis of deeply embedded programs
from functional programs in the theorem prover’s logic
along with equivalence proofs
[
11
,
30
]
.
In the context of formalising complexity theory,
this approach was only used for a lambda calculus model so far
[
10
]
.
In practical verification contexts,
it was used for a lambda calculus model
[
30
]

and assembly
[
29
]
.

2.

Interactively proving properties of deeply embedded programs, mainly using Hoare or other program logics.
This approach is used in many practical verification settings
[
18
,
20
,
16
]

and has been applied to imperative languages, including toy languages
[
32
]
, C, Java, and Rust.
In the context of formalising theoretical computer science, this approach was mainly used for Turing machines
[
39
,
4
,
13
]
,
with few applications to lambda calculus
[
34
,
14
]
and partial recursive functions
[
6
]
.

To formalise the theory of efficient algorithms and complexity theory,
we suggest the synthesis into a simple, imperative, and reasonable computation model.

Why?

As others have noted
[
15
,
11
]
, reasoning about programs in a theorem prover’s native language is significantly easier than reasoning about deeply embedded programs.
This is crucial when verifying complex algorithms.
For example, Gäher and Kunze
[
15
]

proved the Cook-Levin theorem with native Coq programs,
benefiting from existing tools and automation.
In contrast, Balbach
[
5
]

directly reasoned about Turing machines in Isabelle/HOL, requiring extensive low-level reasoning.
Others
[
27
]
note difficulties when reasoning about deeply-embedded programs, e.g. missing warning messages and no static type checking.

Although automated synthesis methods exist for lambda calculi, we believe it is worthwhile to develop them for an imperative model.
The main reason is that the lambda calculi used in previous work
are
less equivalent
to Turing machines than imperative models:
they are only reasonable for computations solving decision problems but not reasonable in space in general
[
12
]
.
This limits their applicability when analysing complexity-theoretic questions.

Finally, while there exists a synthesis-based method for x86-assembly in Coq
[
29
]
,
it does not support user-defined datatypes, only allows pattern matching and recursion with fixed recursion schemes,
and its target language’s semantics are vastly more complicated than needed for complexity-theoretic questions.

Contribution

We present a framework that
synthesises verified programs in a simple while-language that is reasonable (
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
) w.r.t. time and space in Isabelle/HOL.
Our framework takes tail-recursive, first-order functions
1
1

1

Although the framework does not support higher-order functions,
each first-order instance of such a function can be compiled as described in
Section

4
.
with algebraic datatypes as input.
We call this fragment
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}}
.
Given a
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}}
function
f
,
our proof-producing metaprogram
synthesises a related
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}}
function which only operates on natural numbers.
We call this
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}}
fragment
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
.

The resulting
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
function
f^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
is
compiled into a program
p
of our deeply embedded, imperative language
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
,
which supports function calls and tail-recursion.
We provide automation to interactively prove the correspondence between
f^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
and
p
.
In all examples we tried, the proofs were automatic.
Finally, a verified chain of compilers in Isabelle/HOL translates
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
programs into programs in our target computation model
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
.

Fig.

1
gives an overview.
The blow-up introduced by each compiler is linear in the execution time.

Furthermore, we verify a compiler for
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
programs that compiles them into programs in
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
, a computation model with fixed bit-width registers.
This is a step towards showing that our framework and computation model are suited for formalising significant theorems in complexity theory, like the Cook-Levin Theorem, many of which need bit-blasting into a bounded bit-width computation model as a major proof step.
We show that this compilation step is correct and leads to a running blow-up linear in the product of the
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
program running time and the size of the input.

\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}}
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
proof-producing metaprogram
interactive deep embedding
verified compilers
verified compilers

Figure 1
:
Compilation pipeline from
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}}
functions to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
programs.

The target language only has one-bit-wide registers and only allows comparison to zero as well as bit assignments.
Both operations take constant time.
This is in addition to the regular program constructs of if-then-else and while-loops.
Hence it is clear that
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
is reasonable with respect to memory and time.

2.1
,

,
5.2
, and
5.3

and all underlying sub-results are formalised in Isabelle/HOL.
Other proofs are provided in the appendix.

[

]
or more expressive foundation.

function applications,

f
functions with defining equations

Figure 2
:
Grammar for body
t
of a first-order function
f=\lambda\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}.\,t
with ADTs.

We use several deeply-embedded, deterministic languages based on the
imperative language

\mathsf{IMP}
from Winskel’s book
[
38
]
,
which is well-studied in formal verification
[
35
,
31
]
.
Our
\mathsf{IMP}
languages operate on a
state
of type

\mathsf{string}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{val}
.
Terms of type
\mathsf{string}
are called
registers

and terms of type
\mathsf{val}
are called
values
.
The registers in a program
p
are denoted by
\mathsf{regs}\,\,p
.
As a computation model,
\mathsf{IMP}
allows simple reasoning about space since there is no
indirect memory addressing –
only registers occurring in a program can be accessed.
In our final model,
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
, values are single bits,
making the space usage bounded by
\mathsf{regs}\,\,p
.
We define
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
in
Section

5.3
,
and a summary of all
\mathsf{IMP}
languages can be found in
Appendix

0.D
.
Here, we describe the intermediate languages used during compilation.
They use values of type
\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
and
consist of
atoms
,
(arithmetic) expressions
and
(imperative) commands
.
Every atom is also an arithmetic expression.

We now define the
semantics
.
With
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{s}
and
\llbracket a\rrbracket_{s}
we denote the evaluation (returning a natural number) of an
atom
A
and expression
a
, respectively, under state
s
,
defined by the rules in
Figs.

3(a)
and
3(b)
.

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathtt{C}\,n\rrbracket_{s}\equiv n,\hskip 8.99994pt\llbracket\mathtt{R}\,r\rrbracket_{s}\equiv s\,r

(a)

Evaluation of atoms
A
.

\displaystyle\llbracket A_{1}\otimes A_{2}\rrbracket_{s}\equiv\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{s}\otimes\llbracket A_{2}\rrbracket_{s},\text{ for }\otimes\in\{+,-\}

(b)

Evaluation rules for arithmetic expressions.

\mpr@inferstar*[left=Assign]{s^{\prime}=#1(r\coloneqq\llbracket a\rrbracket_{s})}{(r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}a,s)\Rightarrow^{\mathcal{C}}s^{\prime}}\par\mpr@inferstar*[left=IfT]{s\,r\neq 0\\
(p_{1},s)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}}{(\mathtt{IF}\ r\ \mathtt{THEN}\ p_{1}\ \mathtt{ELSE}\ p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n+\mathcal{C}}s^{\prime}}\par\mpr@inferstar*[left=Seq]{(p_{1},s)\Rightarrow^{n_{1}}s^{\prime}\\
(p_{2},s^{\prime})\Rightarrow^{n_{2}}s^{\prime\prime}}{(p_{1}\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n_{1}+n_{2}+\mathcal{C}}s^{\prime\prime}}\par\mpr@inferstar*[left=IfF]{s\,r=0\\
(p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}}{(\mathtt{IF}\ r\ \mathtt{THEN}\ p_{1}\ \mathtt{ELSE}\ p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n+\mathcal{C}}s^{\prime}}

(c)

Execution relation for commands,
where each
\mathcal{C}
is a fixed, non-negative constant.

Figure 3
:
Semantics shared by our
\mathsf{IMP}
-languages, excluding the final
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
.

With

tp\vdash(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}_{R}s^{\prime}

we denote that in context of the program
tp
,
3
3

3

The context is only relevant for recursive calls in
Section

4
.

the program
p
started from
s
terminates in at most
n
steps with state

s^{\prime\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}\,r=s^{\prime\prime}\,r
for all
r\in R
:

\displaystyle\big(tp\vdash(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}_{R}s^{\prime}\big)\equiv\exists n^{\prime}\,s^{\prime\prime}.\;tp\vdash(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n^{\prime}}s^{\prime\prime}\land\forall r\in R.\,s^{\prime}\,r=s^{\prime\prime}\,r\land n^{\prime}\leq n,

where
\Rightarrow^{n}
is the
(big-step) execution relation
,
defined inductively using the rules from
Fig.

3(c)
along with the language-specific rules specified in their respective sections.
In rule
(c)Execution relation for commands,
where each

C

is a fixed, non-negative constant.

,
the state
s
is updated at register
r

using the state update notation
#1(r\coloneqq v)\equiv\lambda r^{\prime}.\,\mathsf{if}\,r=r^{\prime}\,\mathsf{then}\,v\,\mathsf{else}\,s\,r^{\prime}
.
If we are only interested in a single return register
r
, we write

\displaystyle\big(tp\vdash(p,s)\Rightarrow_{r}v\big)\equiv\exists s^{\prime}.\;\big(tp\vdash(p,s)\Rightarrow s^{\prime}\land s^{\prime}\,r=v\big).

To simplify notation, we
omit “
\dots\vdash
” if the context is irrelevant,

R
if the program terminates exactly with state
s^{\prime}
,
and
n
if the number of steps is irrelevant.

One may question why rule
(c)Execution relation for commands,
where each

C

is a fixed, non-negative constant.

only takes constant, and not logarithmic, time.
This is merely for simplicity and makes no difference for polynomial time considerations since
logarithmic space usage (i.e. space usage in a binary encoding of
\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
)
is bounded by time in all intermediate
\mathsf{IMP}
languages, as shown in
Theorem

2.1
.
For this, let
a_{\text{max}}
denote the largest constant in an expression
a
,

p_{\text{max}}
the largest constant in a program
p
,
and
s_{\text{max}}\equiv\text{max}\,\{s\,r\mid r:\mathsf{string}\}
.

Theorem 2.1

If
\text{max}\,\{s_{\text{max}},p_{\text{max}}\}<2^{w}
and

(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}
,
then
s^{\prime}_{\text{max}}<2^{w+n}
.

Proof sketch.

The proof is by induction over the execution relation.
In case of an assignment, the maximum size can at most double.
In other cases, the claim holds by applying the induction hypothesis, followed by algebraic manipulation.
∎

3
\mathrm{HOL}^{(\mathrm{TC})}
to
\mathrm{HOL}^{(\mathrm{TC})\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}

This section describes
the proof-producing translation of
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}}
functions into
equivalent
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
functions.
More generally, our translation applies to any
\mathrm{HOL}
function
f
with ADTs,
producing a
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
function
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}

that operates only on natural numbers
while preserving tail-recursiveness and first-order applications.

Intuitively, the function
f
and its translation
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
should compute the same values for the same inputs,
up to encoding and decoding.
Our method is based on a technique called
transport
, which, intuitively, takes
objects of one type and derives that “corresponding” objects exist of another type,
synthesising the latter objects as necessary.
To formalise this correspondence, we first review some concepts on relations.

Definition 1
.

A
relation on
\mathsf{\alpha}
and
\mathsf{\beta}
is a function of type

\mathsf{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\beta}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{bool}
.
Two functions
f,g
are
related from
R
to
S
if they map
R
-related inputs to
S
-related outputs,
written
(R\Rrightarrow S)\,f\,g\equiv\forall x\,y.\,R\,x\,y\longrightarrow S\,(f\,x)\,(g\,y)
.
Repeated relations are abbreviated as

(R^{n}\Rrightarrow S)\equiv{}({R\Rrightarrow\dotsb\Rrightarrow R}\Rrightarrow S)
, i.e.
R
repeated
n
-times.

We can now define the desired relation between
f
and
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
.

Definition 2
.

A type
\mathsf{\alpha}
is
encodable
if there are functions

\mathsf{natify}:\alpha\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}

and
\mathsf{denatify}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\alpha}

such that

\mathsf{denatify}\,(\mathsf{natify}\,x)=x
for all
x
.
4
4

4

In the formalisation,
\mathsf{natify},\mathsf{denatify}
are overloaded as part of a typeclass.

We say that
n:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
and
x:\mathsf{\alpha}
are
(
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-)related

if they represent the same value:

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,n\,x\equiv n=\mathsf{natify}\,x
.
A number
n
is
well-encoded (with respect to
\mathsf{\alpha}
)

if
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,n\,x
holds for some
x:\mathsf{\alpha}
.
We lift this relation to functions
and say that
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}:\mathsf{\alpha}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
and
f:\mathsf{\alpha}
are
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related

if
\mathsf{\alpha}^{\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,f
,
where
\alpha^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}},\alpha^{\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
are defined recursively:

\displaystyle(\mathsf{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\beta})^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\equiv\mathsf{\alpha}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\beta}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}},\quad\mathsf{\alpha}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\equiv\mathsf{\mathbb{N}},\qquad(\mathsf{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\beta})^{\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\equiv\mathsf{\alpha}^{\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{\beta}^{\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}},\quad\mathsf{\alpha}^{\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\equiv\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}.

Objective 1
.

Given a
\mathrm{HOL}
function
f
,
synthesise a
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
term
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
.
Moreover, the synthesis preserves tail-recursiveness and first-order applications on well-encoded inputs.

We achieve this in two steps:
First, we encode the datatypes used by
f
(
Section

3.1
).
Second, we synthesise a
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
term
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
related to
f
(
Section

3.2
).

3.1
Encoding of Datatypes

Let
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathsf{\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathsf{\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern Following Gordon’s approach
[
17
]
to encode the type of lists,
each value
C_{i}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}
is encoded as a tagged and nested pair.
Datatypes are encoded recursively this way, using natural numbers as the base type:

Definition 3
.

Fix an injective pairing function
\mathsf{pair}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}

with inverses
\mathsf{fst},\mathsf{snd}
, i.e.
\mathsf{fst}\,(\mathsf{pair}\,n\,m)=n

and

\mathsf{snd}\,(\mathsf{pair}\,n\,m)=m
.
We define
C_{i}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{i}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
by

C_{i}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}\equiv\mathsf{pair}\,i\,\big(\mathsf{if}\,a_{i}=0\,\mathsf{then}\,0\,\mathsf{else}\,(\mathsf{pair}\,x_{1}\,(\mathsf{pair}\,x_{2}(\dotsb(\mathsf{pair}\,x_{a_{i}-1}\,x_{a_{i}})\dotsb)\big)
.
For each
i,j
, we define the
selector

\mathsf{select}_{i,j}\,x\equiv(\mathsf{if}\,j<i\,\mathsf{then}\,\mathsf{fst}\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{id})\,(\mathsf{snd}^{j}\,x)
.
Using the selector,
we can define a companion
\mathsf{case}_{d}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}

for the case combinator
\mathsf{case}_{d}

using a nested if-then-else construction.
Details can be found in
Section

0.A.1
.

These functions suffice to show that
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathsf{\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern .

and relations

, we have

\displaystyle(\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{i}}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}})\,C_{i}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,C_{i},\qquad\qquad\,\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,n\,(C_{i}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \displaystyle\big((\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{1}}\Rrightarrow R)\Rrightarrow\dotsb\Rrightarrow(\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{n}}\Rrightarrow R)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\Rrightarrow R\big)\,\mathsf{case}_{d}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,\mathsf{case}_{d}.

The described pipeline is fully automatic in our framework.
An example application can be found in
Section

0.A.1
.
We note that our underlying pairing function is the Cantor pairing function, which results in a number computable in time polynomial in the sizes of the two numbers in the encoded pair.

3.2
Synthesis of
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
Functions

Let
f=\lambda\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}.\,t

be a
\mathrm{HOL}
function of type
\mathsf{\alpha}
.
We aim for
1
.
For this, we can assume that all ADTs in
\alpha
are encoded
and every user-specified function
g\neq f
in
t

is already compiled – and thus
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related to some
g^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
.

Our approach combines
black-box and white-box transports

[
22
,
37
,
19
]

in a novel way, to the best of our knowledge.
Both kinds of transport take a term
t:\mathsf{\alpha}
and a relation
R
as input
and synthesise a term
t^{\prime}
together with a proof that
R\,t^{\prime}\,t
.
While black-box transports do so using only the structure of
\mathsf{\alpha}
and
R
,
white-box transports also use the structure of
t
.

Our approach is as follows:

1.

Use black-box transport to obtain a function
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
that is
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related to
f
.

2.

Use white-box transport to obtain a term
(\lambda\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern The use of black-box transports relies on the fact that
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
can be used as a relation by

]
:

Theorem 3.2

(Blackbox-Transport
[
22
]
)

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathsf{natify}
, and
\mathsf{denatify}
form a partial Galois equivalence.

f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
that is
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related to
f
.

In the formalisation, we use the

prototype
[

to obtain
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
.

3.2
may not preserve tail-recursiveness and first-order applications.

2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle s\,\mathsf{arg}_{f,i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle s\,\mathsf{arg}_{f,i}\hfil$\crcr}}}

whenever

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,(s\,\mathsf{arg}_{f,i})\,x_{i}\hfil

.

The deep embedding has two steps:

1.

Compile
f^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
into an
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
program
p
(
Section

4.1
) and

2.

prove the equivalence between
f^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
and
p
using custom-built automation (
Section

4.2
).
The automation uses symbolic execution,
normalises the (otherwise incomprehensible) program state such that it becomes amenable to automatic proof,
and discharges well-encodedness side-conditions.

Note on Higher-Order Functions

Since
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
does not support higher-order functions,
we assume first-order functions as input for our framework.
Nevertheless, each first-order instance of a higher-order function can be compiled systematically with our framework.
We demonstrate this by means of an example.
More cases can be found in the formalisation:

Example 1

Consider the iteration function
of type
(\mathsf{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\alpha})\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle f^{n}\,x\equiv\mathsf{case}\,n\,\mathsf{of}\,0\Rightarrow x\mid n+1\Rightarrow f^{n}\,(f\,x).

(33)

This function cannot be compiled as it is.
However, every first-order instance,
e.g.
\mathsf{snd}^{j}
as used in the definition of
\mathsf{select}_{i,j}

(
Definition

3
),
can be compiled.
To do so, we define
\mathsf{pow}\mathsf{snd}\,n\equiv\mathsf{snd}^{n}
.
We then instantiate
Eq.

33
with
f=\,\mathsf{snd}

and fold the definition of
\mathsf{pow}\mathsf{snd}
to obtain

\mathsf{pow}\mathsf{snd}\,n\,x=\mathsf{case}\,n\,\mathsf{of}\,0\Rightarrow x\mid\allowbreak n+1\Rightarrow\mathsf{pow}\mathsf{snd}\,n\,(\mathsf{snd}\,x)
.
This equation can then be compiled with our framework.

4.1
Compilation to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}

We compile
f^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\hfil$\crcr}}}=t^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}

to an
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
program
p
in two steps.
For ease of notation, we drop the superscript and just write
f
and
t
below.

First, we
parse
t
into a metaprogram datatype resembling
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
.
7
7

7

While the datatype allows recursive calls in non-tail positions, the compiler rejects such terms using a syntactic check.

Second, we compile the parsed term to an
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
program, as shown in
Fig.

6
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\llbracket\textbf{If}{}\,t_{1}\,t_{2}\,t_{3}\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x}\seqi\mathtt{IF}\ x\ \mathtt{THEN}\ {\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\ \mathtt{ELSE}\ {\llbracket t_{3}\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}&\text{(fresh $x$)}\\
{\llbracket\textbf{Let}{}\,t_{1}\,t_{2}\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x}\seqi{\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket}^{x\#b}_{r}&\text{(fresh $x$)}\\
{\llbracket\textbf{LetBound}{}\,n\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}b\,{!}\,n&\\
{\llbracket\textbf{Arg}{}\,n\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}\mathsf{arg}_{f,n}&\\
{\llbracket\textbf{Number}{}\,n\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}n&\\
{\llbracket\textbf{Call}{}\,g\,[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m}]\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x_{1}}\seqi{\dotsc}\seqi{\llbracket t_{m}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x_{m}}\seqi&\text{(fresh $x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m}$)}\\
&\;\mathsf{arg}_{g,1}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}x_{1}\seqi\dotsc\seqi\mathsf{arg}_{g,m}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}x_{m}\seqi&\\
&\;\mathtt{CALL}\ g^{\mathsf{IMP}}\ \mathtt{RETURN}\ \mathsf{ret}_{g}\seqi r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}\mathsf{ret}_{g}&\text{($g^{\mathsf{IMP}}$ registered for $g$)}\\
{\llbracket\textbf{Recurse}{}\,[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{k}]\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x_{1}}\seqi{\dotsc}\seqi{\llbracket t_{k}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x_{k}}&\text{(fresh $x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}$)}\\
&\;\mathsf{arg}_{f,1}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}x_{1}\seqi\dotsc\seqi\mathsf{arg}_{f,k}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}x_{k}\seqi\mathtt{RECURSE}\hskip-10.00002pt&\end{aligned}

Figure 6
:
The compiler from the
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
representation to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
.
Term
t_{1}
in
\textbf{If}{}\,t_{1}\,t_{2}\,t_{3}
denotes the condition
t_{1}\neq 0
,

\textbf{Let}{}\,t_{1}\,t_{2}
binds
t_{1}
to the first de Bruijn index in
t_{2}
,

\textbf{LetBound}{}\,i
denotes the variable bound by the
i
-th enclosing
Let
, and

\textbf{Arg}{}\,i
the
i
-th argument of
f
, i.e.
x_{i}
.

We denote the compilation by
{\llbracket t\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}
,
where
b
is a list of registers holding values of terms bound by enclosing
Let
bindings and

r
is the register that will hold the program’s result.

For a call
\textbf{Call}{}\,g\,[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m}]
,
the compiler retrieves the registered
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
implementation
g^{\mathsf{IMP}}
of
g
.
For the primitives equality, addition, and subtraction,
manual implementations must be provided to the compiler.
The compiler generates fresh register names when needed, namely
to compile
Let
bindings,
to store the value of
If
conditions,
and to save function arguments in temporary registers to prevent them from being overwritten.
For example, in
\textbf{Call}{}\,g\,[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m}]
,
argument registers could be overwritten if another call of
g
appears within
t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m}
.

In summary, we parse
t
into a term
t^{\prime}
of the metaprogram datatype
and compile
t^{\prime}
to the
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
program
p\equiv{\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket}^{[]}_{\mathsf{ret}_{f}}
.

4.2
Correctness Proofs

Since the compiler to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
is a metaprogram,
its correctness cannot be proven in Isabelle/HOL.
Instead, the correctness of each compiled program
p
must be verified individually through automation
(a process sometimes called “certified extraction”), which we describe next.

First,
p
is normalised such that no recursive constructor (sequences and if-then-elses) appears on the left of a sequence.
This simplifies the implementation, but shall not concern us any further
(for details see
Section

0.B.2
).
The automation proceed as follows:

1.

If
f
is recursive, start an induction proof on

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

using the induction rule of
f
.

2.

Symbolically run
p
to completion or until a recursive call is encountered.
Let
s^{\prime}
be the end state.

3.

Run
f^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
to completion with result
r
or until a recursive call
f^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle y\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle y\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle y\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle y\hfil$\crcr}}}
is encountered.

4.

If both computations ran to completion, prove
s^{\prime}\,\mathsf{ret}_{f}=r
.
Otherwise, prove
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle s^{\prime}\,\mathsf{arg}_{f,i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle s^{\prime}\,\mathsf{arg}_{f,i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern and conclude using the inductive hypothesis.

:
State normalisation rules.

To run
p
, we use the execution rules from
Figs.

3(c)
and
5

(specialised to single return registers)
8
8

The specialisation is routine and can be found in

.

and the correctness theorems of subprograms.
Each update to the
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
state is normalised with the rules from
Fig.

7
to

1.

to simplify the proof state for interactive proofs and

2.

to speed up the automation.

The automation eliminates cases where
p
and
f^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
took different execution branches.

Since our correctness theorems are conditional,
the inductive hypothesis and the subprograms’ correctness theorems are conditional too.
Whenever they are used, goals of the form

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,x^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,x
must be solved.
This is done automatically by a resolution-based method that
uses
Definition

1
,
the relatedness theorems of ADTs and functions (
Theorems

3.1
and
3.2
),
and local assumptions.
Note that this is essentially a form of type-checking,
making sure that all involved terms are well-encoded.

Goals after application of inductive hypothesis.

⬇

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}

(
s

"
a
")

a

\land

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}

(
select
2,2

(
s

"
xs
"))

xs

\land

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}

(
Suc

(
s

"
n
"))

(
Suc

n
)

Figure 8
:
Step-by-step correctness proof for
count
N
, described in
Example

2
.

First, the induction rule of
count
is applied,
creating cases for
[]
and
(#)
.
We focus on the latter.
Running
count
IMP
and
count
N

produces two subcases: one for
x

=

a
and one for
x

\neq

a
.
We consider the former.
The new state
s’

=

s
(
r1

:
=

x1
,...)

is obtained from
s
by normalised state updates.
Due to normalisation, we immediately get:

⬇

s’

"
a
"

=

s

"
a
"

\land

s’

"
xs
"

=

select
2,2
(
s

"
xs
")

\land

s’

"
n
"

=

Suc
(
s

"
n
")

Thus, the inductive hypothesis can be applied, leaving a set of relatedness goals.
These are solved automatically,
using the local assumptions,
the selector relatedness theorems for lists,
and the relatedness theorem for
Suc
.

Case Studies

We applied our framework to typical functional programs,
including standard datatype functions (
\mathsf{map}
,
\mathsf{fold}
,…),
the bisection method for square roots,
and problems from complexity theory –
such as reductions from
SAT
to
3SAT
and
3SAT
to independent sets.
The examples can be found in the formalisation.
All proofs were automatic, but functions using hundreds of registers
had to be split due to inefficient retrievals for the tracked
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
state in the current implementation.
Performance improvements are future work.
We conjecture that our method is complete when applied to programs compiled from
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}}
using our framework,
but a formal proof is out of scope.

5
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}

In this section, we describe the verified chain of compilers from
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
:

Objective 3
.

For every
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
program
p
,
compile an
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
program
p^{\prime}
such that (up to encoding and decoding), for the same input,

1.

p
and
p^{\prime}
compute the same output and

2.

the number of steps
p^{\prime}
takes is at most polynomially more than the number of steps
p
takes.

Note that since the size of the values stored in registers is governed by
Theorem

2.1
, if the above objective is satisfied,
the memory used by the final
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
program is polynomial in the size of
p
and its running time.

The compilation proceeds in several steps:
First, we compile recursive calls into while-loops of an intermediate language
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{WC}}
(
Section

5.1
),
Second, we eliminate calls to other programs (
Section

5.2
).
Finally, we use bit blasting to compile into our final language
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
(
Section

5.3
).

5.1
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{WC}}

In the first step, we replace tail-recursive calls by while-loops,
whose semantics are given in
Fig.

9
.

\mpr@inferstar*[left=WhF]{s\,r=0}{(\mathtt{WHILE}\ r\ \mathtt{DO}\ p,s)\Rightarrow^{\mathcal{C}}s}\hskip 3.00003pt\mpr@inferstar*[left=WhT]{s\,r\neq 0\quad(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n_{1}}s^{\prime}\quad(\mathtt{WHILE}\ r\ \mathtt{DO}\ p,s^{\prime})\Rightarrow^{n_{2}}s^{\prime\prime}}{(\mathtt{WHILE}\ r\ \mathtt{DO}\ p,s)\Rightarrow^{n_{1}+n_{2}+\mathcal{C}}s^{\prime\prime}}

Figure 9
:
Execution relation for
\mathtt{WHILE}
.

In rule
9Execution relation for

WHILE

.
,
the loop-condition
r\ \mathtt{\neq 0}
is checked

before
stepping into the
\mathtt{WHILE}
.
In contrast, tail-recursive calls occur at the
end
of the program.
To bridge this gap, we prove the following rules for
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
.

Lemma 1

Let
(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}(s^{\prime},b)

denote the modified execution relation shown in
Fig.

10
that runs

p
but stops and flags whenever a tail-recursive call is encountered using
b\in\{\mathtt{0},\mathtt{1}\}

\mpr@inferstar*[]{s\,r\neq 0\\
(p_{1},s)\Rightarrow^{n}(s^{\prime},b)}{(\mathtt{IF}\ r\ \mathtt{THEN}\ p_{1}\ \mathtt{ELSE}\ p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n+\mathcal{C}}(s^{\prime},b)}\par\mpr@inferstar*[]{(p_{1},s)\Rightarrow^{n_{1}}(s^{\prime},\mathtt{0})\\
(p_{2},s^{\prime})\Rightarrow^{n_{2}}(s^{\prime\prime},b)}{(p_{1}\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n_{1}+n_{2}+\mathcal{C}}(s^{\prime\prime},b)}\par\mpr@inferstar*[]{}{(\mathtt{RECURSE},s)\Rightarrow^{\mathcal{C}}(s,\mathtt{1})}\par\mpr@inferstar*[]{s^{\prime}=#1(r\coloneqq\llbracket a\rrbracket_{s})}{(r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}a,s)\Rightarrow^{\mathcal{C}}(s^{\prime},\mathtt{0})}\par\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\ldots}

Figure 10
:
Execution relation from
Lemma

1
that halts with a flag when encountering a tail-recursive call. Omitted rules are routine and listed in

Section

0.C.1
.

Then the following rules are admissible:

\mpr@inferstar*[]{(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}(s^{\prime},\mathtt{0})}{tp\vdash(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}}\par\mpr@inferstar*[]{(p,s_{1})\Rightarrow^{n_{1}}(s_{2},\mathtt{1})\\
tp\vdash(tp,s_{2})\Rightarrow^{n_{2}}s_{3}}{tp\vdash(p,s_{1})\Rightarrow^{n_{1}+n_{2}}s_{3}}

Note the similarity between these rules and those for
9Execution relation for

WHILE

.
and
9Execution relation for

WHILE

.
.
These rules suggest a straightforward translation from
p
to an
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{WC}}
program
by using a loop that continues as long as a recursive call would have occurred:

Theorem 5.1

Let
\mathtt{cnt}
be a fresh register with respect to
\mathsf{regs}\,\,p
.
Let
p[t/x]
denote the syntactic substitution of any occurrence of
x
by
t
in a program
p
.
Define

\displaystyle\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{\circlearrowleft}\equiv\mathtt{cnt}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}\mathtt{1}\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}\mathtt{WHILE}\ \mathtt{cnt}\ \mathtt{DO}\ \mathtt{cnt}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}\mathtt{0}\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}p[(\mathtt{cnt}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}\mathtt{1})/\mathtt{RECURSE}].

Then it holds that

p\vdash(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}_{\mathsf{regs}\,p}s^{\prime}
if and only if
(\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{\circlearrowleft},s)\Rightarrow^{n+\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{regs}\,p}s^{\prime}
.

Since we use an additional register, we only get partial equivalence of states in the correctness theorem.
This is sufficient for our purposes nonetheless.

5.2
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{WC}}
to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}

We next eliminate calls to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
programs.
We simply inline every call to a program
p^{w}

by copying its used registers to fresh memory locations,
executing the program there, and copying the result register back:

Definition 5
.

Let
\mathsf{regs}\,\,p^{w}=\{r_{1},\dotsc,r_{n}\}
and
m
be a renaming to fresh registers.
We define the inlining of a call to program
p^{w}
with return register
r
as

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\mathtt{CALL}\ p^{w}\ \mathtt{RETURN}\ r\rrparenthesis_{\star}\equiv m\,r_{1}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}r_{1}\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}\dotsb\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}m\,r_{n}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}r_{n}\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}p^{w}[m\,x/x]\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}m\,r.

For an
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{WC}}
program
p
, we let
\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{\star}
denote the
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
program obtained from
p
by inlining all calls.

Note that called programs must already be compiled to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
and hence do not contain further calls.
Arguing about a program in a different memory location requires the obvious substitution lemma:

Lemma 2

(p[(m\,x)/x],s)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}
implies
(p,s\circ m)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}\circ m

for injective
m
.

The inlining induces a blow-up that is dependent on the program size (but not the input), as shown below.

Theorem 5.2

(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}_{\mathsf{regs}\,\,p}s^{\prime}
if
(\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{\star},s)\Rightarrow^{f(n,p)}_{\mathsf{regs}\,\,p}s^{\prime}

where
f(n,p)
is linear in
n\cdot\lvert p\rvert
.
Moreover, if
(\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{\star},s)\Rightarrow^{n}_{\mathsf{regs}\,\,p}s^{\prime}
then
(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}_{\mathsf{regs}\,\,p}s^{\prime}
.

Note that the fact that
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
is reasonable w.r.t. time and space follows from standard reductions in computability theory.
A good exposition is in Erk and Priese’s book
[
9
]
.
There it is shown that While-programs can be simulated by Goto-programs with constant space (one-to-one-correspondence between registers) and polynomial time overheads (Theorem 12.4.3), and Goto-programs can be simulated with constant space (one tape per register) and polynomial time overheads by
k
-tape Turing Machines (Theorem 11.4.1), and
k
-tape TMs can be simulated by single-tape TMs in constant space (
2k
tuple letters per cell) and quadratic running time overheads (Theorem 7.4.5).
The other direction, showing that TMs can be simulated by While-programs is obvious.

5.3
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}

\mpr@inferstar*[]{b\in\{\mathtt{0},\mathtt{1}\}\\
s^{\prime}=#1(r\coloneqq b)}{(r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}b,s)\Rightarrow^{1}s^{\prime}}\par\mpr@inferstar*[]{s\,r\neq\mathtt{0}\\
(p_{1},s)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}}{(\mathtt{IF}\ r\ \mathtt{THEN}\ p_{1}\ \mathtt{ELSE}\ p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n+1}s^{\prime}}\par\mpr@inferstar*[]{(p_{1},s)\Rightarrow^{n_{1}}s^{\prime}\\
(p_{2},s^{\prime})\Rightarrow^{n_{2}}s^{\prime\prime}}{(p_{1}\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n_{1}+n_{2}+1}s^{\prime\prime}}\par\mpr@inferstar*[]{s\,r=\mathtt{0}\\
(p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}}{(\mathtt{IF}\ r\ \mathtt{THEN}\ p_{1}\ \mathtt{ELSE}\ p_{2},s)\Rightarrow^{n+1}s^{\prime}}\par\mpr@inferstar*[]{s\,r=\mathtt{0}}{(\mathtt{WHILE}\ r\ \mathtt{DO}\ p,s)\Rightarrow^{1}s}\hskip 10.00002pt\mpr@inferstar*[]{s_{1}\,r\neq\mathtt{0}\\
(p,s_{1})\Rightarrow^{n_{1}}s_{2}\\
(\mathtt{WHILE}\ r\ \mathtt{DO}\ p,s_{2})\Rightarrow^{n_{2}}s_{3}}{(\mathtt{WHILE}\ r\ \mathtt{DO}\ p,s_{1})\Rightarrow^{n_{1}+n_{2}+2}s_{3}}

Figure 11
:
Execution relation for
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
.

Our programs so far operate on arbitrary-size natural numbers.
In contrast, our final language
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
operates on single bits,
represented as
\mathtt{0}
and
\mathtt{1}
.
The commands of
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
and their semantics are shown in
Fig.

11
.
There are no arithmetic expressions, and all registers represent a single bit.

We use bit-blasting to compile
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
programs
to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
programs.
The bit-blasting is parametrised with a number
w
, indicating the bit-width of the compilation.
Each
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
register
r
is represented as
w+1
fresh
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
registers
r_{0},\dotsc,r_{w}
:
The
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
register
r_{i}
corresponds to the
i
-th bit of the
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
register
r
,
and the register
r_{0}
indicates whether all bits are
\mathtt{0}
.
Arithmetic expressions of
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
are bit-blasted to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
programs.
We showcase the bit-blasting for addition here.
The addition of
x
and
y
in register
r
is compiled to

\displaystyle\mathtt{add}^{w}\,x\,y\,r\equiv\mathsf{copy}^{w}\,a\,x\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}\mathsf{copy}^{w}\,b\,y\mathrel{\mathtt{;}}\mathsf{adder}^{w}\,r,

(34)

where
a,b
are fresh, fixed
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
registers.
The function copies the first
w
bits of
x
and
y
to
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{w}
and
b_{1},\dotsc,b_{w}
, respectively,
which are then added by the function
\mathsf{adder}^{w}\,r
.
The function
\mathsf{adder}^{w}\,r
puts the result of the addition in registers
r_{0},\dotsc,r_{w}
.
It is implemented as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{adder}^{w}\,r\equiv\mathsf{fullAdder}^{1}\,r;\dotsb;\mathsf{fullAdder}^{w}\,r;carry\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}\mathtt{0};zero\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}\mathtt{0},

(35)

where
\mathsf{fullAdder}^{i}\,r
is a program that implements a standard full adder with zero check.
It adds the bits
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
and the carry register
carry
, storing the result in
r_{i}
, the new carry value in
carry
, and stores in
zero

whether the result and the previous
zero
value were
\mathtt{0}
.
The full adder is called for all bits of the addends.
Then
r_{0}
is written, and the
carry
and
zero
registers are reset.

We bit-blast all other arithmetic expressions of
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
in a similar way;
details are in the formalisation.
In the next definition, we fix a maximum bit-width
w
:

Definition 6
.

We denote with
{\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}^{w}_{r}
the bit-blasted version of arithmetic expression
a
stored in register
r
.
For an
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
state
s
, we denote with
{\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis}^{w}
its corresponding
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
state,
i.e.
{\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis}^{w}\,r_{i}=(s\,r)_{i}
for all registers
r
and
i<w
,
where
n_{i}
denotes the
i
-th bit of
n
.

The functional correctness can now be stated as follows:

Lemma 3

If
\text{max}\,\{a_{\text{max}},\llbracket a\rrbracket_{s},s_{\text{max}}\}<2^{w}

then
({\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}^{w}_{r},{\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis}^{w})\Rightarrow^{f(w)}{\llparenthesis#1(r\coloneqq\llbracket a\rrbracket_{s})\rrparenthesis}^{w}
,
where
f(w)
is linear in
w
.

Lastly, we show that the overall runtime blow-up of the bit-blasted program is polynomial.
We first lift the bit-blasting of expressions to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{W}}
programs
p
, denoted by
{\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis}^{w}
.
In
{\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis}^{w}
, arithmetic expressions are replaced with their bit-blasted version
and all other constructs are mapped to their corresponding constructs in the obvious way.
For functional correctness, we get:

Theorem 5.3

If
(p,s)\Rightarrow^{n}s^{\prime}

and
n<w

and
\text{max}\,\{s_{\text{max}},p_{\text{max}}\}\cdot 2^{n}<2^{w}
,
then
({\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis}^{w},{\llparenthesis s\rrparenthesis}^{w})\Rightarrow^{f(n,w)}{\llparenthesis s^{\prime}\rrparenthesis}^{w}
where
f(n,w)
is linear in
n\cdot w
.

Proof sketch.

The proof is by structural induction on the
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{-}}
program
p
.
There are two interesting cases.
The first is the case of assignment, where the theorem follows from Lemma
3
.
The other case is that of composition, in which case the theorem follows from Theorem
2.1
.
All other cases follow from basic properties of program composition and algebraic manipulation of exponents.
∎

6
Discussion

Our work touches the areas of
formalised computability and complexity theory,
algorithm refinements and program synthesis,
and certified compilation.

Notable work in the first area includes Norrish’s
[
34
]

formalisation results, like Rice’s theorem, in a lambda calculus model.
Xu et al.
[
39
]
and Asperti and Ricciotti
[
4
]

proved basic results on Turing machines,
using Hoare-like logics to reason about deeply-embedded machines.
Carneiro
[
6
]
proved basic results using partial recursive functions.
The richest line of work is by Forster
[
10
]
,
modelling the call-by-value lambda calculus, Turing machines, and other models, and proving simulations between them.
Forster and Kunze
[
11
]
introduced a largely automated synthesis framework
from native Coq functions to said lambda calculus model,
including semi-automated time bound proofs.
This was used for serious algorithms, like the Cook-Levin theorem
[
15
]
.
However, their model is not reasonable in space in general.
Our contribution is the first synthesis method into a simple computation model that is reasonable in time and space.

To make our method practical, we used ideas from algorithm refinements and transport-based program synthesis
[
22
,
37
,
19
]
,
introducing a novel synthesis for recursive functions in
Section

3.2
.
Other related approaches are algorithm refinements, which were deeply studied by Lammich
[
23
,
24
,
26
,
25
]
.

In the area of certified compilation, most substantial developments geared towards practical compilers,
such as CakeML
[
30
]
and CompCert
[
28
]
.
We, on the other hand,
we were mainly inspired by the simple and elegant use of
\mathsf{IMP}
languages
[
38
]

in educational material
[
35
,
31
]
,
which is sufficient to formalise the theory of efficient algorithms and complexity theory.

Future Work

Our framework can be extended in several ways,
such as proving time and memory bounds of synthesised programs with respect to some sensible semantics for Isabelle/HOL functions
[
3
]
,
generalising from tail-recursion to general recursion,
reducing the time and memory overhead of the compilers for finer analyses needed in efficient algorithms,
and extending it to richer models of computation,
e.g. probabilistic computation, interactive computation, or online computation.
We plan to improve the framework’s performance
such that it can be applied
to the verification of serious computational objects,
e.g. reductions, like the Cook-Levin theorem
[
8
]
or Karp’s 21 NP-complete problems,
and the verification of running time bounds of complex algorithms in general, e.g. flow algorithms
[
25
,
2
]
or matching algorithms
[
1
]
.
A challenge with our framework, as in any system that synthesises programs using natural numbers as the sole datatype, is the computational overhead from encoding/decoding datatypes into natural numbers.
Without special care, unsuspecting users may discrepancies between the expected and actual running time of synthesised programs.
Methods to smoothly handle that discrepancy would greatly improve the usability of our framework and are a possible future direction.

\mathrm{HOL}^{(\mathrm{TC})}
to
\mathrm{HOL}^{(\mathrm{TC})\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}

2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathsf{\alpha}_{n}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathsf{\alpha}_{n}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathsf{\alpha}_{n}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{\alpha}_{n}\hfil$\crcr}}}
be an algebraic datatype
with
a_{i}\equiv\lvert\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathsf{\alpha}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathsf{\alpha}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mathsf{\alpha}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{\alpha}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\rvert
.
Recall that we require an injective pairing function

\mathsf{pair}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}

and functions

\mathsf{fst},\mathsf{snd}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}

with

\displaystyle\mathsf{fst}\,(\mathsf{pair}\,n\,m)=n\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{snd}\,(\mathsf{pair}\,n\,m)=m.

(36)

Our construction proceeds as follows:

(a)

We define
C_{i}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{i}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
by

\displaystyle C_{i}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}\equiv\begin{cases}\mathsf{pair}\,i\,(\mathsf{pair}\,x_{1}\,(\mathsf{pair}\,x_{2}(\dotsb(\mathsf{pair}\,x_{a_{i}-1}\,x_{a_{i}})\dotsb),&\text{if }a_{i}>0\\
\mathsf{pair}\,i\,0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(b)

For each
i,j
, we define the
selector

\mathsf{select}_{i,j}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{select}_{i,j}\,x\equiv(\mathsf{if}\,j<i\,\mathsf{then}\,\mathsf{fst}\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{id})\,(\mathsf{snd}^{j}\,x).

(45)

(c)

We define

\mathsf{case}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}:\big((\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{1}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\alpha})\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}{\dotsb\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}(\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{n}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\alpha})\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\alpha}}\big)

by

\displaystyle\mathsf{case}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}\,x\equiv{}\begin{cases}f_{i}\,(\mathsf{select}_{a_{i},1}\,x)\dotsb(\mathsf{select}_{a_{i},a_{i}}\,x),&\text{if }i=\mathsf{fst}\,x,1\leq i\leq n-1\\
f_{n}\,(\mathsf{select}_{a_{n},1}\,x)\dotsb(\mathsf{select}_{a_{n},a_{n}}\,x),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(d)

We define
\mathsf{natify}:\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern and

\displaystyle(\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{i}}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}})\,C_{i}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,C_{i},\qquad\,\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,n\,(C_{i}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \displaystyle\big((\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{1}}\Rrightarrow R)\Rrightarrow\dotsb\Rrightarrow(\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{a_{n}}\Rrightarrow R)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\Rrightarrow R\big)\,\mathsf{case}_{d}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\,\mathsf{case}_{d}.

Proof.

We have to show that
\mathsf{denatify}\,(\mathsf{natify}\,x)=x
for all
x
.
This follows directly by definition of
\mathsf{denatify},\mathsf{natify}
and
the fact that
\mathsf{fst},\mathsf{snd}
are inverse to
\mathsf{pair}
and all

0.A.2
Synthesis of
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
Functions

Theorem
.

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathsf{natify}
, and
\mathsf{denatify}
form a partial Galois equivalence.
Thus, there is some
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
that is
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related to
f
.

Proof.

Due to
[
22
, Lemma 2]
, it suffices to show that

1.

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
is right-total and right-unique,

2.

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,n\,x
implies
\mathsf{denatify}\,n=x
, and

3.

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,(\mathsf{natify}\,x)\,x

for all
n,x
.
By
Definition

2
,

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
is right-total and
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,(\mathsf{natify}\,x)\,x
.
Since
\mathsf{natify}
is injective (
Definition

2
),

\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
is also right-unique
and
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\,n\,x
implies
\mathsf{denatify}\,n=x
.

The existence of
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
now follows from
[
22
, Theorem 1 and 2]
.
∎

]

are
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related
and white-box transports preserve tail-recursiveness and first-order applications.

Proof.

The former is shown by structural induction on
t
,
using that
f
is
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related to
f^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
by
Theorem

3.2

and all other functions
g
are
\mathsf{R}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
-related to
g^{\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
by assumption.

The latter follows immediately from
Definition

4
and structural induction on
t
.
∎

Appendix 0.B
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}

0.B.1
Compilation to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}

Fig.

12
shows the datatype representation of
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
as used in the compiler
to
\mathsf{IMP}^{\mathrm{TC}}
, shown in
Fig.

13
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} t\Coloneqq&\;\textbf{If}{}\,t_{1}\,t_{2}\,t_{3}&\text{(if-then-else with natural number condition $t_{1}$)}\\
\mid&\;\textbf{Let}{}\,t_{1}\,t_{2}\quad&\text{(bind $t_{1}$ to the first de Bruijn index in $t_{2}$)}\\
\mid&\;\textbf{LetBound}{}\,i\quad&\text{(variable bound by $i$-th enclosing {Let})}\\
\mid&\;\textbf{Arg}{}\,i\quad&\text{($i$-th argument of $f$, i.e.\ $x_{i}$)}\\
\mid&\;\textbf{Number}{}\,n&\text{(natural number $n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}$)}\\
\mid&\;\textbf{Call}{}\,g\,[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m}]\quad&\text{(call to $g:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\mathrel{\mathsf{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}$ with arguments $t_{1},\dotsc,t_{m}$)}\\
\mid&\;\textbf{Recurse}{}\,[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{k}]\quad&\text{(recursion with arguments $t_{1},\dotsc,t_{k}$)}\end{aligned}

Figure 12
:
Datatype representation of
\mathrm{HOL}^{\mathrm{TC}\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
in the metaprogramming language.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\llbracket\textbf{If}{}\,t_{1}\,t_{2}\,t_{3}\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x}\seqi\mathtt{IF}\ x\ \mathtt{THEN}\ {\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\ \mathtt{ELSE}\ {\llbracket t_{3}\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}&\text{(fresh $x$)}\\
{\llbracket\textbf{Let}{}\,t_{1}\,t_{2}\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x}\seqi{\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket}^{x\#b}_{r}&\text{(fresh $x$)}\\
{\llbracket\textbf{LetBound}{}\,n\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;r\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}b\,{!}\,n&\\

&\;\mathsf{arg}_{g,1}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}x_{1}\seqi\dotsc\seqi\mathsf{arg}_{g,m}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}x_{m}\seqi&\\

{\llbracket\textbf{Recurse}{}\,[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{k}]\rrbracket}^{b}_{r}\equiv&\;{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x_{1}}\seqi{\dotsc}\seqi{\llbracket t_{k}\rrbracket}^{b}_{x_{k}}&\text{(fresh $x_{1},\dotsc,x_{k}$)}\\
&\;\mathsf{arg}_{f,1}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}x_{1}\seqi\dotsc\seqi\mathsf{arg}_{f,k}\mathrel{\mathtt{\leftarrow}}x_{k}\seqi&\\
&\;\mathtt{RECURSE}&\end{aligned}

2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern
